People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Little things at work, like helping someone shower, or just carrying groceries… they take more out of me than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take pleasure in things that usually make you happy, like hobbies or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe a little. I haven’t really been paying close attention to that. I still do things if I’m supposed to, but I guess they don’t feel like much lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in doing things you used to enjoy, or do you still find some pleasure in them, even if it's less than before?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say completely. It’s more like… I can still get a little something out of things sometimes, but it feels muted. Not like it used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Probably about a month… maybe a little more. It’s just been this low, foggy kind of feeling hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough or blaming yourself for things more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I keep thinking about my tío, before he passed. Like I should’ve called more, or gone to see him sooner. I know I can’t change it, but it feels like I failed him, you know… as family.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re no longer getting any pleasure out of things you used to enjoy, or is there still some enjoyment, even if it’s less than before?\n\nPatient: Like I said, not none… I guess there’s still a little enjoyment sometimes. Just not much.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time recently when you felt any pleasure or happiness doing something you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not a specific time, really. Maybe something small here or there, but nothing that stands out.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost almost all pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or is there still some pleasure, even if it’s much less than before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s almost all gone. It’s just… less. A lot less sometimes, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most things you used to care about, or do you still try to do them even if they don’t feel as good as before?\n\nPatient: I still try sometimes, I guess. It just doesn’t feel like it used to, so I don’t always stick with it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re barely able to feel any pleasure at all anymore, even in things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that extreme. I can still feel something sometimes… just not as much, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re not good enough almost every day, even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I keep thinking about my tío before he passed, like I should’ve called more or gone to see him sooner. It just sits there, like I failed him somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you didn’t feel any pleasure at all in anything, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, maybe some days feel pretty flat, but I don’t think I’d say absolutely nothing gives me any pleasure. It’s more like it’s muted.\n\nDoctor: Have there been days recently when nothing at all—absolutely nothing—gave you any pleasure, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say absolutely nothing. Some things still get through a little, just not like before.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time, you’re not really getting any pleasure out of things you used to enjoy, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no. It’s just… some things feel kind of dulled down, but not gone completely.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the pleasure you get from things you used to enjoy is only a little less than before, or has it dropped to the point where you're hardly feeling any pleasure at all most of the time?\n\nPatient: It’s probably just a little less. I mean, I still feel something from things… it’s just not as much as before, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in almost everything you used to care about, so that even doing things you normally enjoy feels like a chore most days?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say almost everything. Some things are harder to get into, but it’s not like everything feels like a chore most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
